Uniform vorticity depletion and inviscid damping for periodic shear flows in the high Reynolds number regime Thanks: HJ and SC are supported in part by NSF grant DMS-1945179. RB is supported in part by NSF grant DMS-2202974
Rajendra Beekie, Shan Chen, Hao Jia

Abstract
. We study the dynamics of the two dimensional Navier-Stokes equations linearized around a shear flow on a (non-square) torus which possesses exactly two non-degenerate critical points. We obtain linear inviscid damping and vorticity depletion estimates for the linearized flow that are uniform with respect to the viscosity, and enhanced dissipation type decay estimates. The main task is to understand the associated Rayleigh and Orr-Sommerfeld equations, under the natural assumption that the linearized operator around the shear flow in the inviscid case has no discrete eigenvalues. The key difficulty is to understand the behavior of the solution to Orr-Sommerfeld equations in three distinct regimes depending on the spectral parameter: the non-degenerate case when the spectral parameter is away from the critical values, the intermediate case when the spectral parameter is close to but still separated from the critical values, and the most singular case when the spectral parameter is inside the viscous layer.



1.
Introduction and main results

Assume that
\nu\in(0,1)
and
\mathfrak{p}\in(2\pi,\infty)
. Consider the equation

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\partial_{t}\omega-\nu\Delta\omega+b(y)\partial_{x}\omega-b^{\prime\prime}(y)\partial_{x}\psi=0,&\\
\Delta\psi=\omega,&\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

for
(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\times[0,\infty)
.

Equation (
1.1
) can be obtained as the linearization of the two dimensional Navier-Stokes equations on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
around the meta-steady state
(b(y),0)
. Alternatively, one can view the shear flow
(b(y),0)
as an exact steady state to the Navier-Stokes equations with a small external forcing
F:=(-\nu b^{\prime\prime}(y),0),y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Equation (
1.1
) was studied classically, in an attempt to explain the onset of turbulence. See for example
[
20
]
for an interesting history on this problem. We refer also to
[
2
,
15
,
41
]
for more recent results.

We note that it is natural to study (
1.1
) on a non-square torus (for our purposes), since on a square torus some of the most interesting shear flows such as Kolmogorov flows are not stable
[
18
]
. To establish asymptotic stability, a more natural object to study on a square torus seems to be the dipole flow, see Remark 1.3 in
[
65
]
.

Our main goal in this paper is to understand the precise dynamics of (
1.1
) starting from relatively smooth initial data, and obtain bounds on the solution that are uniform with respect to the viscosity
\nu\in(0,\nu_{0})
for some small
\nu_{0}>0
. In particular we shall capture some of the
more refined dynamics associated with periodic shear flows, and prove uniform vorticity depletion and linear inviscid damping results. Such problems are well known and were discussed for example in section 11, chapter 6 of the book
[
7
]
by Bedrossian and Vicol .

1.1.
Review of recent results on inviscid damping and main problems

The study of stability problems in mathematical analysis of fluid dynamics has a long and distinguished history, dating back to the work of Kelvin
[
42
]
, Orr
[
52
]
and Rayleigh
[
53
]
among many others, and continuing to the present day. Despite many significant recent progresses, many important questions still remain open. The main issue is that very often the most interesting physical settings involve high Reynolds number (or equivalently the viscosity has to be taken very small), where the Navier-Stokes equations degenerate to Euler equations and the continuous spectrum plays a dominant role. As a starting point to a more complete and precise theory of hydrodynamic stability, it is therefore important to understand mathematically the property of the linearized flow in the high Reynolds number regime, including the limiting inviscid case. Below we briefly review some recent works on the linearized flow for both Euler and Navier-Stokes equations with small viscosity, focusing on two dimensional shear flows which is our main object of study in this paper, and mentioning nonlinear inviscid damping and other extensions only briefly.

1.1.1.
The case of monotonic shear flow

For the Euler equations, there are significant recent progresses on the asymptotic stability of monotonic shear flows and vortices, assuming spectral stability, see for example
[
6
,
27
,
36
,
39
,
40
,
47
,
56
,
62
,
68
,
69
]
for linear results. The main mechanism of stabilization is the so called “inviscid damping”, which refers to the transfer of energy of vorticity to higher and higher frequencies leading to decay of the stream and velocity functions, as
t\to\infty
. Extending the linearized stability analysis for inviscid fluid equations to the full nonlinear setting is a challenging problem, and the only available results are on spectrally stable monotonic shear flows
[
5
,
33
,
34
,
50
]
, and on point vortices
[
35
]
. We refer also to the recent review article
[
37
]
for a more in-depth discussion of recent developments of both linear and nonlinear inviscid damping.

In view of the results on linear inviscid damping for the Euler equation, it is natural to expect that even for Navier-Stokes equations with small viscosity we should have the same inviscid damping results with explicit rates of decay, in time, of the stream functions and velocity fields. There are several results in this direction, all of which are for
monotonic
shear flows, see for example
[
4
,
8
,
13
,
48
]
for the Couette flow with full nonlinear analysis or precise linear results, and
[
27
]
for the spectrally restricted stream function for “mixing layer” type shear flows (but a description of the full solution without spectral restrictions is not available). More recently, Chen, Wei and Zhang
[
15
]
established uniform inviscid damping with quantitative bounds for general spectrally stable monotonic shear flows in a periodic channel. More precisely, they proved that the stream function decays like
1/t
as
t\to\infty
, uniformly in viscosity. We remark that it remains an interesting question if the stream function decays as in the Euler equation (with rate
1/t^{2}
). It seems that one needs to understand the boundary layer more clearly to settle this quesiton. The third author
[
41
]
proved uniform (in viscosity) Gevrey bounds on the profile of the vorticity, for spectrally stable monotonic shear flows in
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
(thus avoiding the boundary layer issue), which yields sharp bounds on the stream function (and in particular
1/t^{2}
decay rate).

1.1.2.
The case of non-monotonic shear flows

Many physically important shear flows are not monotonic, such as Poiseuille flow and Kolmogorov flows. For such flows on the linear inviscid level, there is an additional significant physical phenomenon called “vorticity depletion” which refers to the asymptotic vanishing of vorticity as
t\to\infty
near the critical points of the shear flow, first predicted in Bouchet and Morita
[
9
]
, and proved rigorously in Wei-Zhang-Zhao
[
63
]
. A similar phenomenon for the case of vortices was observed earlier by Bassom and Gilbert
[
3
]
and demonstrated in Bedrossian-Coti Zelati-Vicol
[
6
]
. In
[
36
]
Ionescu and the third author obtained a refined description of the dynamics of the linearized flow around monotonic vortices in Gevrey spaces, which is a critical step towards proving nonlinear vortex symmetrization.

In
[
63
]
by Wei-Zhang-Zhao, sharp linear inviscid damping estimates and quantitative depletion estimates were obtained for an important class of “symmetric shear flows” in a periodic channel (see also
[
65
]
by Wei-Zhang-Zhao for a similar result for Kolmogorov flow). When no symmetry is assumed, Ionescu, Iyer and the third author proved optimal inviscid damping rates and quantitative vorticity depletion estimates in
[
38
]
.

As in the case of monotonic shear flows, it is also natural to consider the linearized equation around non-monotonic shear flows for the Navier-Stokes equations and study the corresponding vorticity depletion and linear inviscid damping estimates that are uniform with respect to the viscosity
0<\nu\ll 1
. This problem is more complicated than the monotonic case and, as far as we know, is completely open, since the corresponding Orr Sommerfeld equations are
genuinely
fourth order singular ODEs and the estimates needed to establish optimal vorticity depletion and linear inviscid damping are very strong. We note also that the Orr-Sommerfeld equations have been studied classically, with a focus on distribution of eigenvalues, see
[
28
,
29
]
and references therein for recent results in this direction. We also mention the essay
[
20
]
for an interesting account on the history of research in this direction. Our main task here is quite different, which is to establish very precise control for the resolvent for the spectral parameter on the continuous spectrum (of the inviscid Euler equation).

1.2.
Main equations and assumptions

In this paper we address this problem and study the uniform vorticity depletion and linear asymptotic stability around a periodic shear flow
(b(y),0)
on
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, together with enhanced dissipation estimates. The choice of the domain is motivated by our goal of understanding the interaction of viscosity and critical points of shear flows, and by the desire to avoid the boundary layers which arise when there is a boundary. We note that the issues of critical points and boundary layers are somewhat decoupled, and for the sake of clarity of argument, it makes sense to tackle these difficulties separately.

Strictly speaking,
(b(y),0)
is not a steady state for the Navier-Stokes equations, and becomes steady state only with a small external force
F=(-\nu b^{\prime\prime}(y),0)
. In our setting, we consider the viscosity
\nu
to be very small. The effect of diffusion on the background shear flow is negligible, at least up to the diffusion time scale
T\ll\frac{1}{\nu}
. After the diffusion time, due to the enhanced dissipation effect, the flow is essentially dominated by a heat evolution, at least heuristically. Therefore our analysis below still captures the main dynamics of the Navier-Stokes equations near shear flows, even when we do not add the external forcing.

1.2.1.
Main spectral assumption on the background shear flow

Our main assumptions on the background flow
(b(y),0),y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
are the following.

Assumption 1.1
.

We assume that the shear flow (b(y),0),
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
satisfies the following conditions:

•

b\in C^{4}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
. The set
S:=\{y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}:b^{\prime}(y)=0\}=\{y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}\}
, and for some
\varkappa\in(0,1)
, we have

|b^{\prime\prime}(y)|\in[\varkappa,1/\varkappa]\,\,{\rm for\,\,}y\in S,\quad{\rm and}\quad\|b\|_{C^{4}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\leq 1/\varkappa.

(1.2)

•

The linearized operator
L_{k}:L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
with
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
defined for
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
as

L_{k}g(y):=b(y)g(y)-b^{\prime\prime}(y)\varphi,\quad{\rm where}\,\,(-k^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2})\varphi(y)=g(y),\,\,y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},

(1.3)

has no eigenvalues, nor generalized embedded eigenvalues (also called singular limiting modes) in
\{b(y),y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\}
.

In contrast to the spectral property of the linearized operator around monotonic shear flows, the spectral property of
L_{k}
is less understood, especially on the mechanism of the generation of discrete eigenvalues and embedded eigenvalues. From general spectral theory, we know that the spectrum of
L_{k}
consists of the continuous spectrum

\Sigma:=\big\{b(y):\,y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\big\},

(1.4)

together with some discrete eigenvalues with nonzero imaginary part which can only accumulate at the set of continuous spectrum
\Sigma
. Unlike the case of monotonic shear flows where the discrete eigenvalues can accumulate only at inflection points of the background shear flow, there appears no simple characterization of the possible accumulation points for non-monotonic shear flows.

Recall that
\lambda\in\Sigma
is called an embedded eigenvalue if there exists a nontrivial
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, such that

L_{k}g=\lambda g.

(1.5)

For non-monotonic shear flows, this definition is too restrictive, as accumulation points of discrete eigenvalues may no longer be embedded eigenvalues. To capture the discrete eigenvalues, we recall the following definition of “generalized embedded eigenvalues”, which can be found already in
[
63
]
, adapted to our setting.

Definition 1.1
.

We call
\lambda\in\Sigma
a generalized embedded eigenvalue, if one of the following conditions is satisfied.

•

\lambda
is an embedded eigenvalue.

•

\lambda\not\in\{b(y_{1\ast}),b(y_{2\ast})\}
and there exists a nontrivial
\psi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}):\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\to\mathbb{C}
such that in the sense of distributions on
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

(k^{2}-\partial_{y}^{2})\psi(y)+{\rm P.V.}\frac{b^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi(y)}{b(y)-\lambda}+i\pi\sum_{z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\,b(z)=\lambda}\frac{b^{\prime\prime}(z)\psi(z)}{|b^{\prime}(z)|}\delta(y-z)=0.

(1.6)

We remark that our assumption that the critical points
y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}
of
b(y)
being non-degenerate implies that the sum in (
1.6
) is finite. The spectral assumption
1.1
is satisfied by a large class of shear flows
(b(y),0)
, including
\sin\frac{2\pi y}{\mathfrak{p}}
.

1.2.2.
Main equations

Taking the Fourier transform in
x
in the equation (
1.1
), we obtain for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
that

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\partial_{t}\omega_{k}+\nu(k^{2}-\partial_{y}^{2})\omega_{k}+ikb(y)\omega_{k}-ikb^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi_{k}=0,&\\
(-k^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2})\psi_{k}=\omega_{k},&\end{array}\right.

(1.7)

for
(y,t)\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\times[0,\infty)
.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
, define the operator
L_{k,\nu}:H^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
as follows. For
g\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
and
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

L_{k,\nu}g(y):=(\nu/k)\partial_{y}^{2}g-ib(y)g+ib^{\prime\prime}(y)\varphi,\quad{\rm with}\,\,(-k^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2})\varphi=g.

(1.8)

We can rewrite equation (
1.7
) more abstractly as

\partial_{t}\omega_{k}^{\ast}=kL_{k,\nu}\omega_{k}^{\ast},\quad{\rm for}\,\,t\geq 0,

(1.9)

where we have set for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
t\geq 0
,

\omega_{k}^{\ast}(t,y)=e^{\nu k^{2}t}\omega_{k}(t,y).

(1.10)

By relatively standard spectral theory and our bounds below (see section
5
for more details), for sufficiently small
\nu_{0}>0,\sigma_{\sharp}>0
and
\alpha\geq-\sigma_{\sharp}|\nu/k|^{1/2}
, (denoting
\iota={\rm sign}\,k
) we have the following representation formula for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
t>0
,

\begin{split}\omega_{k}^{\ast}(t,y)&=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{i\mathbb{R}+\iota\alpha}e^{\mu tk}\big[(\mu-L_{k,\nu})^{-1}\omega_{0k}\big](y)\,d\mu\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\lambda tk+\alpha|k|t}\big[(i\lambda+\iota\alpha-L_{k,\nu})^{-1}\omega_{0k}\big](y)\,d\lambda\\
&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{\alpha|k|t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\lambda tk}\big[(i\lambda-\iota\alpha+L_{k,\nu})^{-1}\omega_{0k}\big](y)d\lambda.\end{split}

(1.11)

In the above,
\omega_{0k}
denotes the
k-
th Fourier coefficient of the initial data
\omega|_{t=0}
.

Define for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\iota={\rm sign}\,k
,

\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha):=\big[(i\lambda-\iota\alpha+L_{k,\nu})^{-1}\omega_{0k}\big](y).

(1.12)

It follows from (
1.12
) that

(i\lambda-\iota\alpha)\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)+L_{k,\nu}\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda)=\omega_{0k}(y),\quad{\rm for}\,\,y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\lambda\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.13)

Therefore
\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda)
satisfies the equation for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
\iota={\rm sign}\,k
,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\Big[\frac{\nu}{k}\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))-\iota\alpha\Big]\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)+ib^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)=\omega_{0k}(y),&\\
(-k^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2})\psi_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)=\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha).&\end{array}\right.

(1.14)

We summarize our calculations in the following proposition.

Proposition 1.1
.

Assume that
\nu\in(0,\nu_{0})
with sufficiently small
\nu_{0}>0
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
. Suppose that
\omega_{k}(t,y)
satisfies the regularity condition
\omega_{k}(t,y)\in C^{\infty}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
and
\omega_{k}(t,\cdot)\in C([0,\infty),L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}))
is the solution to the system of equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\partial_{t}\omega_{k}(t,y)+\nu(k^{2}-\partial_{y}^{2})\omega_{k}(t,y)+ikb(y)\omega_{k}(t,y)-ikb^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi_{k}(t,y)&=0,\\
(-k^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2})\psi_{k}(t,y)&=\omega_{k}(t,y),\end{array}\right.

(1.15)

for
(y,t)\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\times[0,\infty)
, with initial data
\omega_{k}(0,y)=\omega_{0k}(y)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
. Define the operator
L_{k,\nu}:H^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
as follows. For any
g\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
,

L_{k,\nu}g(y)=(\nu/k)\partial_{y}^{2}g-ib(y)g+ib^{\prime\prime}(y)\varphi,\quad{\rm where\,\,}(-\partial_{y}^{2}+k^{2})\varphi=g\,\,{\rm for}\,\,y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}.

(1.16)

Set for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
\iota={\rm sign}\,k
,
\alpha\geq-\sigma_{\sharp}|\nu/k|^{1/2}
with sufficiently small
\sigma_{\sharp}\in(0,1/10)
,

\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)=\big[(i\lambda-\iota\alpha+L_{k,\nu})^{-1}\omega_{0k}\big](y).

(1.17)

Then
\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\Lambda)
satisfies the equation for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\Big[\frac{\nu}{k}\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))-\iota\alpha\Big]\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)+ib^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)=\omega_{0k}(y),&\\
(-k^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2})\psi_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)=\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha).&\end{array}\right.

(1.18)

We have the representation formulae for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},t>0
,

\begin{split}\omega_{k}(t,y)&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{\alpha|k|t}e^{-\nu k^{2}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ik\lambda t}\omega_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)\,d\lambda,\\
\psi_{k}(t,y)&=-\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{\alpha|k|t}e^{-\nu k^{2}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-ik\lambda t}\psi_{k,\nu}(y,\lambda+i\iota\alpha)\,d\lambda.\end{split}

(1.19)

Strictly speaking the vorticity function
\omega_{k}(t,y)
and stream function
\psi_{k}(t,y)
also depend on
\nu
. We omit this dependence from our notations for the sake of simplicity, since there is no danger of confusion.

1.3.
Main results

Our main result is the following theorem.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume the spectral condition
1.1
. Fix
\gamma\in[15/8,2)
. There exist
\sigma_{\sharp}\in(0,1)
and
\nu_{0}\in(0,1/10)
sufficiently small depending on
\gamma
, such that the following statement holds for
\nu\in(0,\nu_{0})
. Assume that

\omega(t,x,y)\in C([0,\infty),H^{3}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}))

with the associated stream function
\psi(t,x,y)
is the unique solution to (
1.1
), with initial data
\omega_{0}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
satisfying for all
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\omega_{0}(x,y)\,dx=0.

(1.20)

Then we have the following bounds.

(i) Uniform inviscid damping estimates:
for
(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\times[0,\infty)
,

|\psi(t,x,y)|\lesssim_{\gamma}\frac{|y-y_{1\ast}|^{\gamma-2}+|y-y_{2\ast}|^{\gamma-2}}{\langle t\rangle^{2}}e^{-\sigma_{\sharp}\nu^{1/2}t}\|\omega_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})},

(1.21)

\begin{split}&|u^{x}(t,x,y)|\lesssim_{\gamma}\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle}e^{-\sigma_{\sharp}\nu^{1/2}t}\|\omega_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})},\\
&|u^{y}(t,x,y)|\lesssim_{\gamma}\frac{|y-y_{1\ast}|^{\gamma-2}+|y-y_{2\ast}|^{\gamma-2}}{\langle t\rangle^{2}}e^{-\sigma_{\sharp}\nu^{1/2}t}\|\omega_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}.\end{split}

(1.22)

(ii) Uniform vorticity depletion estimates: for
j\in\{1,2\}
there exists a decomposition

\omega(t,x,y):=\omega^{j}_{\rm loc}(t,x,y)+\omega^{j}_{\rm nloc}(t,x,y),

(1.23)

where for
(x,y,t)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\times[0,\infty)
,

\begin{split}&|\omega^{j}_{\rm loc}(t,x,y)|\lesssim_{\gamma}\big(|y-y_{j\ast}|+\nu^{1/4}\big)^{\gamma}e^{-\sigma_{\sharp}\nu^{1/2}t}\|\omega_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})},\\
&|\omega^{j}_{\rm nloc}(t,x,y)|\lesssim_{\gamma}\frac{1}{\langle t\rangle^{\gamma/2}}e^{-\sigma_{\sharp}\nu^{1/2}t}\|\omega_{0}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}.\end{split}

(1.24)

In the above,
\langle t\rangle:=\sqrt{1+t^{2}}
, and the implied constants depend, in addition to
\gamma
, also on
\varkappa
from (
1.2
) and the structure constant
\kappa\in(0,1)
from Proposition
4.1
.

We have the following remarks on Theorem
1.1
.

(1)

The exponent
\gamma
is almost sharp. The endpoint case
\gamma=2
seems to require new ideas to treat.

(2)

The restriction that
\mathfrak{p}\in(2\pi,\infty)
is only needed for the spectral assumption
1.1
to hold. An important example that satisfies
1.1
is the Kolmogorov flow
(\sin(2\pi y/\tp),0)
. See also Meshalkin and Sinai
[
55
]
for an investigation of spectral stability of Kolmogorov flows on general torus.

(3)

The condition (
1.20
) removes the zero mode in
x
of vorticity from our results, since the evolution of the zero mode corresponding to
k=0
in equation (
1.15
) is trivial.

(4)

The bounds (
1.21
)-(
1.22
) provide uniform-in-viscosity inviscid damping estimates. We note that to get
1/t^{2}
decay as
t\to\infty
, one needs to use integration by parts in (
1.19
) twice with respect to
\lambda
, while the decay bound on
u^{x}
involves only one integration by parts in
\lambda
and one derivative in
y
. In the neighborhood of the critical points
y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}
, taking a derivative with respect to
\lambda
is roughly equivalent to
\frac{1}{b^{\prime}(y)+i\nu^{1/4}}\partial_{y}
, which explains the unbounded factor
|y-y_{1\ast}|^{\gamma-2}+|y-y_{2\ast}|^{\gamma-2}
in the bounds for
\psi
and
u^{y}
.

(5)

The bounds (
1.24
) confirm the “vorticity depletion” phenomenon which predicts the asymptotic vanishing of vorticity near the critical points
y_{1\ast}
and
y_{2\ast}
as
t\to\infty
. In addition, we note that there is a cut-off distance
\nu^{1/4}
to the vorticity depletion effect in contrast to the Euler equation case, which is natural since the viscous effect dominates in small scales and the dissipation it causes offsets the vorticity depletion over the viscous region.

(6)

We comment on the regularity assumptions on the initial data. By a simple dimension counting argument, we expect that (
1.24
) to be quite sharp. On the other hand, in (
1.21
)-(
1.22
), one might be able to reduce the regularity assumption to
\omega_{0}\in H^{5/2}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
. For the sake of conciseness we have refrained from optimization in this direction.

(7)

It is a natural problem to also consider the problem in
\mathbb{T}\times[0,1]
, to include a boundary. There are essential new difficulties due to the presence of boundary layers. We refer to the recent work
[
2
,
15
]
and references therein for significant results on monotonic shear flows in this direction.
The boundary effect is completely decoupled from the issue of critical points of
b(y)
away from the boundary, and therefore can be treated separately. We plan to address this issue in a future work.

1.4.
Main ideas of the proof

In this section we briefly outline the main ideas of the proof. To present the key elements in the simplest settings, we assume that
k=1
and
\alpha=0
in (
1.18
).

The fact that we can take
\alpha
as
-\sigma_{\sharp}|\nu/k|^{1/2}
is crucial to obtain the enhanced dissipation bound in our approach, which requires that we show stability of our bounds with respect to moving from
\alpha=0
to
\alpha=-\sigma_{\sharp}|\nu/k|^{1/2}
. The higher
k
cases should, in principle, make the problem easier since the problem becomes more “elliptic”. In practice, however, the analysis becomes considerably more complicated as we need to track the dependence on
k
of various quantities, as optimally as possible. Nonetheless these are secondary issues that we neglect here to emphasize the main points.

To further simplify the discussion, we assume also that
\omega_{0k}
vanishes in a neighborhood of
\{y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}\}
. The depletion estimates are still nontrivial and manifest as a repelling effect for the vorticity away from the critical points. The general case can be reduced to a case similar to this.

With the help of the representation formula (
1.19
), it suffices to study the “spectral density functions” for the stream function
\psi(y,\lambda)
and
\omega(y,\lambda)
, see (
1.18
). For the sake of conciseness, we have suppressed the dependence on
\nu
(recall that we assumed
k=1
,
\alpha=0
). Indeed, the uniform-in-viscosity inviscid damping bounds follow from integration by parts in
\lambda
using (
1.19
), and the vorticity depletion bounds follow from weighted estimates on
\psi(y,\lambda)
which imply asymptotic vanishing of
\psi(y,\lambda)
as
y\to\{y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}\}
.

Roughly speaking, the bounds on
\psi(y,\lambda)
are that

\begin{split}&|\psi(y,\lambda)|\sim\big(|\lambda-b(y)|+\min\{|\lambda-b(y_{1\ast})|,|\lambda-b(y_{2\ast})|\}+|\nu|^{1/2}\big)^{\gamma/2},\\
&|\partial_{y}\psi(y,\lambda)|\sim\big(|\lambda-b(y)|+\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\}+|\nu|^{1/2}\big)^{\frac{\gamma-1}{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\Big\langle\log|\frac{\lambda-b(y)}{\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})}\Big|\Big\rangle,\\
&|\partial^{2}_{y}\psi(y,\lambda)|\sim\big(|\lambda-b(y)|+\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\}+|\nu|^{1/2}\big)^{\frac{\gamma-2}{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\bigg|\frac{\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})}{\lambda-b(y)}\bigg|.\end{split}

(1.25)

We also have similar bounds on
\partial_{\lambda}\psi(y,\lambda)
, with the heuristic that in terms of size for
\beta\in\{1,2\}
,

\partial^{\beta}_{\lambda}\psi(y,\lambda)\sim\big(|\lambda-b(y)|+\min\{|\lambda-b(y_{1\ast})|,|\lambda-b(y_{2\ast})|\}+|\nu|^{1/2}\big)^{-\beta/2}\partial^{\beta}_{y}\psi(y,\lambda).

(1.26)

The bounds (
1.25
) show that
\psi(y,\lambda)
becomes very small as
y
approaches the critical points
\{y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}\}
when
\lambda
is close to one of the critical values
\{b(y_{1*}),b(y_{2*})\}
. These decay bounds are crucial for obtaining the explicit depletion rate for the vorticity functions, see (
1.24
). The bounds on
\partial_{y}^{2}\psi(y,\lambda)
and
\partial_{\lambda}^{2}\psi(y,\lambda)
have to be refined near
\{y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}:b(y)-\lambda=0\}
so that they can be integrated, see Lemma
4.1
below.

The first critical step in proving the bounds (
1.25
)-(
1.26
) is to reformulate the equation (
1.18
) as

(-1+\partial_{y}^{2})\psi(y,\lambda)+\Big[\nu\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))\Big]^{-1}\big(ib^{\prime\prime}\psi(\cdot,\lambda)\big)(y)=\Big[\nu\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))\Big]^{-1}\omega_{01}(y),

(1.27)

and to justify the heuristic that

\Big[\nu\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))\Big]^{-1}\approx\frac{1}{i(\lambda-b(y))+\ell_{\nu}(\lambda)},

(1.28)

where

\ell_{\nu}(\lambda):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\nu^{1/3},&{\rm if}\,\,\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\}\gtrsim 1,\\
\min\limits_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\}^{1/3}\nu^{1/3},&{\rm if}\,\,\nu^{1/2}\ll\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\}\ll 1,\\
\nu^{1/2},&{\rm if}\,\,\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\}\lesssim\nu^{1/2}.\end{array}\right.

(1.29)

To rigorously show (
1.28
) in various senses, we prove pointwise estimates for the kernel of
\Big[\nu\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))\Big]^{-1}
, see Proposition
3.1
, as well as obtain refined characterizations of the (quantitative) singular behavior near
\{y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}:b(y)-\lambda=0\}
. The factor
\ell_{\nu}(\lambda)
in (
1.29
) is determined by the kernel estimates.

To study (
1.27
), we use (
1.28
) and obtain the following approximate equation for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

(-1+\partial_{y}^{2})\psi(y,\lambda)+\frac{ib^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi(y,\lambda)}{i(\lambda-b(y))+\ell_{\nu}(\lambda)}\approx\frac{\omega_{01}(y)}{i(\lambda-b(y))+\ell_{\nu}(\lambda)}.

(1.30)

As in
[
38
]
, depending on how close
\lambda
is to the critical values
\{b(y_{1\ast}),b(y_{2\ast})\}
, the nonlocal term
\frac{ib^{\prime\prime}(y)\psi(y,\lambda)}{i(\lambda-b(y))+\ell_{\nu}(\lambda)}
plays very different roles.

•

Case I: non-degenerate region
\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\}\gtrsim 1
. In this case, the nonlocal term can be viewed as a compact perturbation of the main part
-1+\partial_{y}^{2}
of the equation and can be treated using our spectral assumptions in
1.3
.

•

Case II: the intermediate region
\nu^{1/2}\ll\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\}\lesssim 1
. In this case, as observed in
[
36
]
and
[
38
]
, assuming for concreteness that
\nu^{1/2}\ll|\lambda-b(y_{1\ast})|\ll 1
, we have by simple computations that for
|\lambda-b(y_{1\ast})|^{1/2}\ll|y-y_{1\ast}|\ll 1
, the nonlocal term is

\frac{ib^{\prime\prime}(y)}{i(\lambda-b(y))+\ell_{\nu}(\lambda)}\approx-\frac{2}{|y-y_{1\ast}|^{2}},

(1.31)

which is both
critically singular
and
favorable
. This term can not be considered as a perturbation. In section
2.2
we incorporate this term into the main part (in addition to
-1+\partial_{y}^{2}
) and study the associated Green’s functions which we call “modified Green’s function” as in
[
36
,
38
]
. After taking off the non-perturbative part of the nonlocal term, the rest of the terms can be treated perturbatively, using the spectral assumptions in
1.3
.

•

Case III: the viscous region
\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\}\lesssim\nu^{1/2}
. As is expected, the viscosity dominates in small regions which in our setting limits the depletion effect to a limit length scale. The scale
\ell_{\nu}(\lambda)
is determined by the bounds we obtained for the kernel of
\Big[\nu\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))\Big]^{-1}
. On the technical level, in the viscous region, the analogous computation (
1.31
) is valid only for
|y-y_{1\ast}|\gg|\nu|^{1/4}
, where we note that the lower bound is independent of
\lambda
. This cutoff is ultimately responsible for the factor
\nu^{1/4}
that appeared in (
1.24
).

After identifying the main terms using the above analysis, we then show that the resulting equations can be solved, by proving suitable limiting absorption principle in weighted Sobolev spaces which are designed to capture both the regularity and vanishing of the spectral density functions
\psi(y,\lambda)
.

In order to prove the limiting absorption principle, we reformulate the left hand side of (
1.30
) as

\psi(y,\lambda)+T_{\Theta}\psi(y,\lambda)

(1.32)

where
T_{\Theta}
is defined based on whether
\lambda
is in the non-degenerate or intermediate/viscous region (see (
4.1
) and (
4.2
)). Written in this way, the limiting absorption principle can then be understood as the statement that “
I+T_{\Theta}
is invertible” in suitable weighted Sobolev spaces. To establish this, we proceed via a contradiction argument after showing that
T_{\Theta}
is “small” when the frequency is high by proving strong regularity estimates. The contradiction then follows as a consequence of the spectral assumption
1.1
.

An essential difficulty that arises when the viscosity
\nu>0
in contrast to the Euler equation case is that the singularities we need to handle are no longer explicit. The complexity of the singularities can be seen from the kernel of
\Big[\nu\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))\Big]^{-1}
, which although on the level of regularity is comparable with the simpler term in (
1.28
), behaves nonetheless rather differently. Indeed, the kernel is highly oscillatory and does not admit a simple expression.

The lack of explicit formula for the operator
\Big[\nu\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))\Big]^{-1}
presents serious technical challenges especially when estimating
\partial_{\lambda}^{2}\psi(y,\lambda)
since we need to take two derivatives in
\lambda
in equation (
1.27
) and the resulting singularities involve very singular terms of the order
\Big[\nu\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))\Big]^{-3}
. To resolve this issue, instead of taking derivatives in
\lambda
, we use the
good derivative

D_{\lambda}
defined in (
4.9
), which does not worsen the singularities. Using the observation that
\partial_{y}^{\beta}\psi(y,\lambda)
with
\beta\in\{1,2\}
can be understood using the equation (
1.18
) directly, we can then obtain sufficient control on
\partial_{\lambda}^{\beta}\psi(y,\lambda)
for our purposes.

1.5.
Notations and conventions

Here we summarize some important notations and conventions used throughout the rest of the paper, for easy references.

In the sections below we assume that
k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,\infty)
as the case of
k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap(-\infty,-1]
follows by a complex conjugation. Define for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\},m\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,\infty)
, and
h(y)\in H^{m}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
,

\|h\|^{2}_{H^{m}_{k}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}:=\sum_{j=0}^{m}|k|^{2(m-j)}\|\partial_{y}^{j}h\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}^{2}.

(1.33)

Below we list some notations that we reserve for important quantities and briefly explain their roles.

•

Fundamental parameters
\lambda,\,\nu,\,k,\,\alpha,\,\epsilon,\,j
:
These parameters are directly given by the equations as in Proposition
1.1
.
\nu\in(0,1)
is the viscosity and will be fixed throughout the paper.
k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,\infty)
denotes the Fourier mode in
x
. We sometimes also use
\epsilon:=\nu/k\in(0,1)
to simplify notations.
We will use
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
to denote our main spectral parameter, and
\alpha\geq-\sigma_{\sharp}|\nu/k|^{1/2}
as an auxiliary spectral parameter which are useful when we shift the contour of integral, as in Proposition
1.1
, where
\sigma_{\sharp}\in(0,1)
will be specified below.
j\in\{1,2\}
is used to specify the the critical point
y_{j\ast}
under consideration.

•

Derived parameters
\delta_{0},\,\delta_{1}(\lambda),\,\delta_{2}(\lambda),\,\Sigma_{j,\delta_{0}},\,S^{j}_{d}
:
These parameters are introduced to simplify notations. It follows from Assumption
1.1
that there exists a
\delta_{0}\in(0,1/8)
such that

\begin{split}&|y_{1\ast}-y_{2\ast}|>10\delta_{0},\sup_{y\in\cup_{y_{\ast}\in\{y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}\}}(y_{\ast}-4\delta_{0},y_{\ast}+4\delta_{0})}|b^{\prime\prime\prime}(y)|\delta_{0}<|b^{\prime\prime}(y_{\ast})|/10.\end{split}

(1.34)

Define

\begin{split}\delta_{1}(\lambda):=&8\min_{y_{\ast}\in\{y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}\}}\sqrt{|\lambda-b(y_{\ast})|/b^{\prime\prime}(y_{\ast})},\\
\delta_{2}(\lambda):=&\frac{1}{8}\min_{y_{\ast}\in\{y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}\}}\sqrt{|\lambda-b(y_{\ast})|/b^{\prime\prime}(y_{\ast})},\end{split}

(1.35)

and for
j\in\{1,2\}
the set

\Sigma_{j,\delta_{0}}:=\Big[b(y_{j\ast})-|b^{\prime\prime}(y_{j\ast})|\delta_{0}^{2}/16,\,b(y_{j\ast})+|b^{\prime\prime}(y_{j\ast})|\delta_{0}^{2}/16\Big].

(1.36)

We introduce also for
j\in\{1,2\},d\in(0,1/10)
the shorthand

S^{j}_{d}:=[y_{j\ast}-d,y_{j\ast}+d].

(1.37)

•

Composite notations
\Lambda,\Theta
:
Below we use the composite symbol
\Lambda
and
\Theta
to indicate dependence of certain quantities on
\Lambda:=(\lambda,\alpha,\epsilon)
(or
\Lambda=(\lambda,\alpha,k,\nu)
recalling that
\epsilon=\nu/k
). For example,
h(y;\Lambda)
denotes a function
h
of
y
that depends on the parameters
\lambda,\alpha
and
\epsilon
. We use
\Theta:=(\lambda,\alpha,\epsilon)
(or
\Theta=(\lambda,\alpha,k,\nu)
) for similar purposes. The motivation for using both
\Lambda
and
\Theta
with identical purpose is purely aesthetic.

•

Weights
: To capture the precise depletion phenomena, we need to prove various weighted estimates. For this purpose, define

\delta(\Lambda):=|\alpha|^{1/2}+C^{\dagger}|\epsilon|^{1/4}+8\min_{y_{\ast}\in\{y_{1\ast},y_{2\ast}\}}\sqrt{|\lambda-b(y_{\ast})|/b^{\prime\prime}(y_{\ast})}.

(1.38)

The constant
C^{\dagger}\gg 1
depends only on
\delta_{0}
and will be chosen below in Proposition
7.1
.
For
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
j\in\{1,2\}
with

\delta(\Lambda)\leq\mathfrak{p}/8,

(1.39)

we define the weights for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\varrho_{j}(y;\Lambda):=|y-y_{j\ast}|+\delta(\Lambda),\quad\varrho_{j,k}(y;\Lambda):=\varrho_{j}(y;\Lambda)\wedge\frac{1}{k},\quad d_{j,k}:=\delta(\Lambda)\wedge\frac{1}{k}.

(1.40)

In the above,
\alpha\wedge\beta
is the minimum of two real numbers
\alpha,\beta
.

•

Weighted spaces
: For
\mathfrak{M}:=(\lambda,\alpha,k,\nu,j)
and
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the weighted space
X^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\mathfrak{M})
using the norm for any function
g
on
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\begin{split}\|g\|_{X^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}(\mathfrak{M})}:=&\sum_{\beta\in\{0,1\}}(\delta(\Lambda))^{-1/2+\sigma_{1}}\Big\|\big[\delta(\Lambda)\wedge\frac{1}{k}\big]^{\sigma_{2}+\beta}\partial_{y}^{\beta}g\Big\|_{L^{2}(S^{j}_{\delta(\Lambda)})}\\
&+\sum_{\beta\in\{0,1\}}\Big\|\varrho_{j}^{\sigma_{1}}(\cdot;\Lambda)\varrho_{j,k}^{\sigma_{2}+\beta}(\cdot;\Lambda)\partial^{\beta}_{y}g\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash S^{j}_{\delta(\Lambda)})}.\end{split}

(1.41)

Lastly we remark that all the implied constants are uniform with respect to the parameters
\lambda,\alpha,k,\nu
and only depend on the
\varkappa
from (
1.2
) and the structure constant
\kappa
from Proposition
4.1
in subsection
4.2
.

1.6.
Organization of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.

•

In section
2
we study the Green’s function and modified Green’s function for the main elliptic operators from the Rayleigh equation.

•

In section
3
we prove pointwise bounds on the kernel of the inverse for the generalized Airy operators that appear in the Orr-Sommerfeld equations, and use them to bound the inverse of the generalized Airy operators in various weighted spaces.

•

In section
4
we state the limiting absorption principle and use them to derive the main bounds on the spectral density functions.

•

In section
5
we prove our main result, Theorem
1.1
.

•

In sections
6
and
7
we give the proof of the limiting absorption principles assuming various bounds on
T_{\Theta}
.

•

In section
8
we prove the technical propositions needed on
T_{\Theta}
for the proofs of the limiting absorption principles.

2.
Bounds on the Green’s function and modified Green’s function

In this section we recall and prove some useful estimates for Green’s functions associated with Rayleigh equations, including the Green’s function for the standard ellitpic operator
k^{2}-\partial_{y}^{2}
on
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and for the elliptic operator
k^{2}-\partial_{y}^{2}+V(y)
on
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
where
V
is a potential term coming from the nonlocal term in the Rayleigh equations. Throughout the section we assume that
k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,\infty)
.

2.1.
Elementary properties of the standard Green’s function

Recall that the Green’s function
G_{k}(y,z)
solves for
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

-\frac{d^{2}}{dy^{2}}G_{k}(y,z)+k^{2}G_{k}(y,z)=\delta(y-z).

(2.1)

G_{k}
has the symmetry

G_{k}(y,z)=G_{k}(z,y),\qquad{\rm for}\,\,y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}.

(2.2)

We note the following point-wise bounds for
G_{k}
with
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

|G_{k}(y,z)|+|k|^{-1}|\partial_{y}G_{k}(y,z)|\lesssim|k|^{-1}e^{-|k||y-z|},

(2.3)

and the integral bounds

\begin{split}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\beta\in\{0,1\}}\bigg[|k|^{3/2-\beta}\left\|\partial_{y,z}^{\beta}G_{k}(y,z)\right\|_{L^{2}(z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\bigg]\lesssim 1.\end{split}

(2.4)

Define
F_{k}(y,z)
for
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
as

F_{k}(y,z):=\partial_{z}\partial_{y}G_{k}(y,z)-\delta(y-z),\qquad{\rm for}\,\,y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}.

(2.5)

Then
F_{k}
satisfies the bounds

\begin{split}&\sup_{y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\beta\in\{0,1\}}\bigg[|k|^{-1/2-\beta}\left\|\partial_{y,z}^{\beta}F_{k}(y,z)\right\|_{L^{2}(z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\bigg]\lesssim 1.\end{split}

(2.6)

These estimates are well known and follow also from the argument below in section
2.2
.

2.2.
Bounds on the modified Green’s function

For applications below, we need to study the “modified Green’s function”
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)
defined for the parameters
j\in\{1,2\},\lambda\in\Sigma_{j,\delta_{0}}
, and
\delta(\Lambda)\leq 1/8
, which satisfies for
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},

(k^{2}-\partial_{y}^{2})\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)+\frac{b^{\prime\prime}(y)}{b(y)-\lambda-i\alpha}\Big[\varphi_{0}\big(\frac{y-y_{j\ast}}{\delta_{0}}\big)-\varphi_{0}\big(\frac{y-y_{j\ast}}{\delta(\Lambda)}\big)\Big]\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)=\delta(y-z).

(2.7)

In the above,
\varphi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(-2,2)
with
\varphi_{0}\equiv 1
on
[-1,1]
.

The bounds we need for the modified Green’s function
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)
are the following.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)
be defined as in (
2.7
) for
j\in\{1,2\},\lambda\in\Sigma_{j,\delta_{0}}
and
\delta(\Lambda)\leq 1/8.

Then we have for
\beta\in\{0,1\}
and
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)=\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(z,y;\Lambda)
, and

\varrho_{j,k}^{\beta}(y;\Lambda)\big|\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)\big|\lesssim\varrho_{j,k}(z;\Lambda)\min\Big\{e^{-|k||y-z|},\frac{\varrho_{j}^{2}(y;\Lambda)}{\varrho_{j}^{2}(z;\Lambda)},\,\frac{\varrho_{j}(z;\Lambda)}{\varrho_{j}(y;\Lambda)}\Big\}.

(2.8)

As a corollary of Lemma
2.1
, we also have the following bounds.

Lemma 2.2
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
2.1
,
define for
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\mathcal{H}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda):=\Big[\partial_{z}+\varphi_{0}\big(\frac{y-y_{j\ast}}{10\delta(\Lambda)}\big)\partial_{y}\Big]\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda).

(2.9)

Then we have the bounds for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},z\in S^{j}_{4\delta(\Lambda)}
and
\beta\in\{0,1\}
,

\begin{split}&\varrho_{j,k}^{\beta}(y;\Lambda)\big|\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{H}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)\big|\lesssim\min\Big\{e^{-|k||y-z|},\frac{\varrho_{j}^{2}(y;\Lambda)}{\varrho_{j}^{2}(z;\Lambda)},\,\frac{\varrho_{j}(z;\Lambda)}{\varrho_{j}(y;\Lambda)}\Big\},\end{split}

(2.10)

and for
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\varrho_{j,k}^{\beta}(y;\Lambda)\big|\partial_{y}^{\beta}(\partial_{z}+\partial_{y})\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)\big|\lesssim\min\left\{e^{-(|k|/2)|y-z|},\frac{\varrho_{j,k}(y;\Lambda)}{\varrho_{j}(z;\Lambda)},\frac{\varrho_{j,k}(z;\Lambda)}{\varrho_{j}(y;\Lambda)}\right\}.

(2.11)

The rest of the subsection is devoted to the proof of Lemma
2.1
and Lemma
2.2
.

2.2.1.
Proof of Lemma
2.1

For simplicity, we suppress the dependence of
\mathcal{G}_{k}^{j}
on
z,\Lambda
,
k
,
j
which are fixed, and set for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

h(y):=\mathcal{G}_{k}^{j}(y,z;\Lambda),\quad V(y):=\frac{b^{\prime\prime}(y)}{b(y)-\lambda-i\alpha}\Big[\varphi_{0}\big(\frac{y-y_{j\ast}}{\delta_{0}}\big)-\varphi_{0}\big(\frac{y-y_{j\ast}}{\delta(\Lambda)}\big)\Big].

We also set for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\varrho(y):=\varrho_{j}(y;\Lambda),\quad\varrho_{k}(y):=\varrho_{j,k}(y;\Lambda),\quad y_{\ast}:=y_{j\ast}.

We separate the proofs into three steps, corresponding to different configurations of
y
and
z
. With a slight abuse of notation we shall prove the bounds (
2.8
) for
y=y_{1}\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and use
y
as a free variable.

Step 1: local estimates near
z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.
Multiplying
\overline{h}
to (
2.7
) and integrating over
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we get that

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}h(y)|^{2}+|k|^{2}|h(y)|^{2}dy+\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}V(y)|h(y)|^{2}dy=\bar{h}(z).

(2.12)

Notice that
\real V(y)\geq 0
and for
y\in S^{j}_{\delta_{0}}
satisfying

|y-y_{*}|\gg\delta(\Lambda),

that

1+\real V(y)\gtrsim\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}(y)}.

(2.13)

It follows from (
2.12
) that for large
C_{0}>1
,

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}h(y)|^{2}+|k|^{2}|h(y)|^{2}dy+\int_{y\in S^{j}_{\delta_{0}},|y-y_{*}|>C_{0}\delta(\Lambda)}\frac{1}{\varrho^{2}(y)}|h(y)|^{2}dy\lesssim|h(z)|.

(2.14)

Using the inequality that

\|h\|_{L^{\infty}(J)}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{2}(J_{*})}|J|^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\|\partial_{y}h\|_{L^{2}(J)}|J|^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.15)

for any interval
J,\ J_{*}
with
J_{*}\subseteq J
and
|J_{*}|\gtrsim|J|
, and choosing the interval

J=\Big\{y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}:|y-z|\leq 2C_{0}\min\{1/|k|,\varrho(z)\}\Big\},

we obtain that

|h(z)|\lesssim\min\{1/|k|,\varrho(z)\}.

(2.16)

It follows from Sobolev type inequality and (
2.14
) that

\sup_{y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\,|y-z|\leq\min\{\varrho(z),1/|k|\}}|\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\lambda+i\alpha)|\lesssim\min\{\varrho(z),1/|k|\}.

(2.17)

Then, by (
2.7
) we also obtain that

\sup_{y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\,|y-z|\leq\min\{\varrho(z),1/|k|\}}|\partial_{y}\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)|\lesssim 1.

(2.18)

Step 2: Global bounds (I) large
k
case.

In this step we prove, under the assumption that

1/k\leq\varrho_{j}(z;\Lambda),

(2.19)

for
\beta\in\{0,1\}
and
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\varrho_{j,k}^{\beta}(y;\Lambda)\big|\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\lambda+i\alpha)\big|\lesssim\varrho_{j,k}(z;\Lambda)e^{-|k||y-z|}.

(2.20)

We will remove the assumption (
2.19
) at the end of the proof.

Let
\varphi\in C^{1}_{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, the space of piecewise
C^{1}
functions, with
\varphi(z)=0
, to be chosen more precisely below. We multiply
\varphi^{2}\overline{h}
to (
2.7
) and integrate over
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
to get that

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\varphi(y)\partial_{y}h(y)|^{2}dy+\left(k^{2}+V(y)\right)|\varphi(y)h(y)|^{2}dy+\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}2\varphi^{\prime}(y)\varphi(y)\overline{h}(y)\partial_{y}h(y)dy=0.

(2.21)

Taking the real part of (
2.21
), using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
\real V(y)\geq 0
we obtain that

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\Big[|\varphi^{\prime}(y)|^{2}-|k|^{2}|\varphi(y)|^{2}\Big]|h(y)|^{2}dy\geq 0.

(2.22)

We assume, without loss of generality that
y_{1}
lies in the positive direction to
z
counterclockwise, and that the distance from
z
to
y_{1}
is greater than
1/k
(otherwise the desired bound (
2.20
) follows from (
2.17
)). We now specify
\varphi
as follows

\begin{split}&\varphi(z)=0,\quad\varphi^{\prime\prime}(y)=0\quad\text{for}\ y\in\left[z,z+c/k\right]\backslash\{0\},\quad\varphi|_{y\in\{z-c/k,z+c/k\}}=1,\\
&\varphi^{\prime}(y)=k\varphi(y)\quad\text{for}\ y\in\left[z+c/k,y_{1}-c/k\right],\quad\varphi^{\prime}(y)=0\quad\text{for}\ y\in\left[y_{1}-c/k,z+\mathfrak{p}/2\right].\end{split}

(2.23)

In the above
0<c<1
is a small constant. And then extend
\varphi
to
[z-\mathfrak{p}/2,z]
by an even reflection across
z
. We denote
I=[y_{1}-c/k,y_{1}+c/k]
, then

\varphi(y)\approx e^{|k||y_{1}-z|}
for
y\in I
and

\int_{I}|h(y)|^{2}dy\lesssim e^{-2|k||y_{1}-z|}\int_{z-c/k}^{z+c/k}|h(y)|^{2}dy.

(2.24)

By equation (
2.7
) and a local energy inequality, we obtain with
I^{\prime}:=[y_{1}-c/(2k),y_{1}+c/(2k)]
,

\int_{I^{\prime}}\varrho^{-2}(y)|h(y)|^{2}+|\partial_{y}h(y)|^{2}dy\lesssim k^{2}\int_{I}|h(y)|^{2}dy.

(2.25)

By (
2.15
), we obtain that for
y\in I^{\prime}
,

|h(y)|\lesssim\varrho_{k}(z)e^{-|k||y_{1}-z|}.

(2.26)

The rest of the bounds in (
2.20
) then follow from equation (
2.7
) and (
2.26
).

Step 3: Global estimates (II) large
V
case.

In this step we prove, under the assumption that

1/k\geq\varrho_{j}(z;\Lambda),

(2.27)

the bounds for
\beta\in\{0,1\}
and
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\varrho_{j,k}^{\beta}(y;\Lambda)\big|\partial_{y}^{\beta}\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)\big|\lesssim\varrho_{j,k}(z;\Lambda)\min\Big\{\frac{\varrho_{j}^{2}(y;\Lambda)}{\varrho_{j}^{2}(z;\Lambda)},\,\frac{\varrho_{j}(z;\Lambda)}{\varrho_{j}(y;\Lambda)}\Big\}.

(2.28)

We will remove the assumption (
2.27
) at the end of the proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume the critical point
y_{j*}=0
, and consider only the main case
y_{1},z\in[0,\delta_{0}]
and
z>\delta(\Lambda)
. In addition, we assume that
y_{1}>z
, the other case following from the symmetry of the modified Green’s function.

Multiplying
\varphi^{2}\overline{h}
to (
2.7
) and integrating over
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\varphi(y)\partial_{y}h(y)|^{2}+\left(k^{2}+V(y)\right)|\varphi(y)h(y)|^{2}dy+\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}2\varphi^{\prime}(y)\varphi(y)\overline{h}(y)\partial_{y}h(y)\,dy=0.

(2.29)

Set for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\displaystyle h(y)=r(y)h^{*}(y),

(2.30)

where
r(y)\in C^{1}_{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
is positive and is chosen to satisfy the following properties

r(y)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}|y|^{1/2}&\text{on}\ [-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]\backslash J,\ J:=[-z,z],\\
(\delta_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}&\text{on}\,\ \mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}].\end{array}\right.

(2.31)

From (
2.29
) and (
2.30
) we obtain that

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\frac{1}{r^{2}(y)}\Big[(r^{2}(y)\varphi^{\prime}(y))^{2}-\left(r^{2}(y)|r^{\prime}(y)|^{2}+r^{4}(y)\real V(y)\right)\varphi^{2}(y)\Big]|h^{\ast}(y)|^{2}dy\\
&\geq-\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varphi^{2}(y)|h^{\ast}(y)|^{2}(r^{\prime}(y)r(y))^{\prime}dy.\end{split}

(2.32)

and therefore for
\varphi
satisfying
\varphi\equiv 0
on
[-z,z]
,

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}\backslash J}\frac{1}{r^{2}(y)}\Big[(r^{2}(y)\varphi^{\prime}(y))^{2}-\left(r^{2}(y)|r^{\prime}(y)|^{2}+r^{4}(y)\real V(y)\right)\varphi^{2}(y)\Big]|h^{*}(y)|^{2}dy\geq 0.

(2.33)

We notice the pointwise bounds for
y\in[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]\backslash J
and some
C_{2}>1
that

m^{2}(y):=r^{2}(y)|r^{\prime}(y)|^{2}+r^{4}(y)\real V(y)\geq\max\left\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{9}{4}-C_{2}\frac{\delta(\Lambda)^{2}}{y^{2}}-C_{2}|y|\right\}.

(2.34)

It is clear that there exists a
y_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
such that

\int_{[z,y_{1}-\varrho(y_{1})/8]}|m(y)|dy=\int_{[y_{1}^{\prime}+\varrho(y_{1}^{\prime})/8,-z]}|m(y)|dy.

(2.35)

In the above, we identify
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
with the circle of circumference
\mathfrak{p}
, and for
a,b\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
where
b
is in the positive direction of
a
(counterclockwise),
[a,b]
denotes the interval from
a
to
b
.

We now specify
\varphi\in C^{1}_{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
as the following

\displaystyle\varphi|_{[-z,z]}=0,\quad\varphi^{\prime\prime}(y)=0,\quad y\in[-z-c\varrho(z),z+c\varrho(z)]\backslash[-z,z],\quad\varphi|_{\{-z-c\varrho(z),z+c\varrho(z)\}}=1;

\displaystyle r(y)\varphi^{\prime}(y)=-\Big[r^{2}(y)|r^{\prime}(y)|^{2}+r^{4}(y)\real V(y)\Big]^{1/2}\varphi(y),\quad y\in[y_{1}^{\prime}+\varrho(y_{1}^{\prime})/8,-z];

(2.36)

\displaystyle r(y)\varphi^{\prime}(y)=\Big[r^{2}(y)|r^{\prime}(y)|^{2}+r^{4}(y)\real V(y)\Big]^{1/2}\varphi(y),\quad y\in[z,y_{1}-\varrho(y_{1})/8];

(2.37)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(y)=0,\ y\in[y_{1}-\varrho(y_{1})/8,y_{1}^{\prime}+\varrho(y_{1}^{\prime})/8].

(2.38)

From the definition of
\varphi
, we obtain the bound for
y\in[y_{1}-\varrho(y)/8,y_{1}+\varrho(y)/8]
for a small
c>0
,

\varphi(y)\geq c\Big[\varrho(y_{1})/\varrho(z)\Big]^{3/2}.

(2.39)

Then the bound (
2.33
) and the definiton (
2.2.1
) imply that

\begin{split}&\Big[\varrho(z)/\varrho(y_{1})\Big]^{3}\int_{[y_{1}-\varrho(y_{1})/8,y_{1}+\varrho(y_{1})/8]}\frac{1}{r^{2}(y)}|h^{\ast}(y)|^{2}dy\\
&\lesssim\int_{[z-c\varrho(z),z+c\varrho(z)]}\varrho^{-2}(z)|h(y)|^{2}dy\lesssim\varrho(z).\end{split}

(2.40)

The desired bounds (
2.28
) then follow from (
2.40
), equation (
2.7
) and local energy inequality. We omit the routine details.

Step 4: Removing assumptions (
2.19
) and (
2.27
).
We consider first the assumption (
2.19
). The key is to establish the bound

k\int_{z-c/k}^{z+c/k}h^{2}(y)dy\lesssim\varrho^{2}(z),

(2.41)

in the case that
1/k\geq\varrho(z)
. We can use the bound (
2.28
) and then (
2.41
) follows.

We now turn to the assumption (
2.27
). The key is to prove the bound

\int_{[z-c\varrho(z),z+c\varrho(z)]}\varrho^{-1}(z)|h(y)|^{2}dy\lesssim 1/k^{2},

(2.42)

in the case that
1/k\leq\varrho(z)
. In this case, we can use the bound (
2.20
) and (
2.42
) follows.

Lemma
2.1
is now proved.

2.2.2.
Proof of Lemma
2.2

This proof of (
2.10
) is almost the same as the one in
[
38
]
. We briefly outline the main ideas below.

Denote for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\varphi^{{\dagger}}(y):=\varphi_{0}\Big(\frac{y-y_{j*}}{10\delta(\Lambda)}\Big).

(2.43)

By direct computation, we see that
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{j}(y,z;\Lambda)
satisfies for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\begin{split}&(k^{2}-\partial_{y}^{2})\mathcal{H}_{k}^{j}(y,z;\Lambda)+V(y)\mathcal{H}_{k}^{j}(y,z;\Lambda)\\
&=-2\partial_{y}\varphi^{{\dagger}}(y)\partial_{y}^{2}\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)-\partial_{y}^{2}\varphi^{{\dagger}}(y)\partial_{y}\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)-\varphi^{{\dagger}}(y)\partial_{y}V(y)\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)\\
&:=F(y,z;\Lambda).\end{split}

(2.44)

Notice the inequality that for
z\in S^{j}_{4\delta},y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

|F(y,z;\Lambda)|\lesssim\max\Big\{|k|(\delta(\Lambda))^{-1},(\delta(\Lambda))^{-2}\Big\}e^{-|k||y-z|},\quad\ \text{Supp}\,F\subseteq[-20\delta(\Lambda),20\delta(\Lambda)].

Then the desired bound (
2.10
) follows from (
2.8
).

We now turn to the proof of (
2.11
). Notice that
(\partial_{y}+\partial_{z})\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)
satisfies the equation for
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\Big[k^{2}-\partial_{y}^{2}+V(y)\Big](\partial_{y}+\partial_{z})\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda)=-\partial_{y}V(y)\mathcal{G}^{j}_{k}(y,z;\Lambda).

(2.45)

The desired bounds (
2.11
) then follow from (
2.8
).

3.
Pointwise bounds on the kernel of generalized Airy operators and applications

In this section we consider the generalized Airy operator
\epsilon\partial_{y}^{2}-\alpha+i(\lambda-b(y))
, where
b(y)
satisfies Assumption
1.2
,
\epsilon\in(0,1),\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha\geq-\sigma_{0}\epsilon^{1/2}
with a sufficiently small
\sigma_{0}\in(0,1)
. Our main goal is to prove pointwise bounds for the kernel of the generalized Airy operator and then obtain estimates on various operators involving the generalized Airy operator.

3.1.
Pointwise estimates on the kernel of generalized Airy operators

Depending on the value of
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we divide into three regions.

(1)

the non-degenerate region:
\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|b(y_{j*})-\lambda|\}\gtrsim 1
;

(2)

the intermediate region:
\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|b(y_{j*})-\lambda|\}\ll 1
;

(3)

the viscous region:
\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|b(y_{j*})-\lambda|\}\lesssim\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Assume that
b(y)
satisfies Assumption
1.2
and that
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Then there exist
\sigma_{0},c_{0}\in(0,1)
such that the following statement holds.

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, denote
\Lambda:=(\epsilon,\lambda,\alpha)
and let
k(y,z;\Lambda)
be the fundamental solution to the generalized Airy operator
\epsilon\partial_{y}^{2}-\alpha+i(\lambda-b(y))
on
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, where
\alpha\geq-\sigma_{0}\epsilon^{1/2}
. More precisely, for
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, in the sense of distributions,

\epsilon\partial_{y}^{2}k(y,z;\Lambda)-\alpha k(y,z;\Lambda)+i(\lambda-b(y))k(y,z;\Lambda)=\delta(y-z).

(3.1)

We have the following bounds for
k(y,z;\Lambda)
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

(1)

if
\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|b(y_{j*})-\lambda|\}\gtrsim 1
, then

\begin{split}|k(y,z;\Lambda)|\lesssim&\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{3}}}{\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}(b(z)-\lambda)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
&\times\exp\left[-c_{0}\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}(b(y)-\lambda),\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}(b(z)-\lambda)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}|y-z|\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}\right];\end{split}

(3.2)

(2)

if
\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|b(y_{j*})-\lambda|\}\ll 1
, then

\begin{split}|k(y,z;\Lambda)|\lesssim&\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{2+\beta}{3}}}{\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}(b(z)-\lambda)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
&\times\exp\left[-c_{0}\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\alpha,(b(y)-\lambda)\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}},(b(z)-\lambda)\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}|y-z|\epsilon^{-\frac{1-\beta}{3}}\right];\end{split}

(3.3)

where
\beta
is determined by
\min_{i\in\{1,2\}}\{|b(y_{i*})-\lambda|\}\approx\epsilon^{2\beta}
,
\beta\in[0,1/4]
.

(3)

if
\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|b(y_{j*})-\lambda|\}\lesssim\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then

\begin{split}|k(y,z;\Lambda)|\lesssim&\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{4}}}{\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}(b(z)-\lambda))\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}\\
&\times\exp\left[-c_{0}\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}(b(y)-\lambda),\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}(b(z)-\lambda)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}|y-z|\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}\right].\end{split}

(3.4)

The rest of the subsection is devoted to the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
Without loss of generality, below we only consider the case
\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\{|b(y_{j*})-\lambda|\}=|b(y_{1*})-\lambda|
, and denote
y_{*}=y_{1*}
for simplicity. Through the rest of this section, we also denote
k(y,z)=k(y,z;\Lambda)
for the ease of notations.

3.1.1.
Global energy estimates and bounds at
z

We first use the global energy estimates to obtain bounds on
k
at
y=z
.

Lemma 3.1
.

For all
z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have the following bounds.

(1)

if
|b(y_{*})-\lambda|\gtrsim 1
,

|k(z,z)|\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{3}}}{\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{3}}(b(z)-\lambda)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(3.5)

(2)

if
\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll|b(y_{*})-\lambda|\lesssim 1
,

|k(z,z)|\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{2+\beta}{3}}}{\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}(b(z)-\lambda)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(3.6)

(3)

if
|b(y_{*})-\lambda|\lesssim\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
,

|k(z,z)|\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{-\frac{3}{4}}}{\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}(b(z)-\lambda)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

(3.7)

Proof.

We first prove the degenerate case (2).
Multiplying (
3.1
) with
\overline{k(y,z)}
and
(\lambda-b(y))\overline{k(y,z)}
respectively, and integrating over
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we get that

-\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy+i\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda-b(y))|k(y,z)|^{2}dy=\overline{k(z,z)};

(3.8)

\begin{split}-\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda-b(y))|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy+\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}b^{\prime}(y)\partial_{y}k(y,z)\overline{k(y,z)}dy-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda-b(y))|k(y,z)|^{2}\\
+i\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda-b(y))^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy=(\lambda-b(z))\overline{k(z,z)}.\end{split}

(3.9)

It follows from (
3.8
) that

\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy+\alpha\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\leq|k(z,z)|.

(3.10)

Similarly, it follows from (
3.9
) that

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda-b(y))^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\leq|\lambda-b(z)||k(z,z)|+\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|b^{\prime}(y)||\partial_{y}k(y,z)||k(y,z)|dy

(3.11)

Using the spectral gap inequalities

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|b^{\prime}(y)|^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\lesssim\epsilon^{-\frac{2-2\beta}{3}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda-b(y))^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy+\epsilon^{\frac{2+4\beta}{3}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy,

(3.12)

and

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\lesssim\epsilon^{-\frac{2+4\beta}{3}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(\lambda-b(y))^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy+\epsilon^{\frac{2-2\beta}{3}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy,

(3.13)

we get that for any
\tau\in(0,1)
and a corresponding
C(\tau)\in(1,\infty)
,

\begin{split}&\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|b^{\prime}(y)|\cdot|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|\cdot|k(y,z)|dy\\
&\leq\epsilon^{\frac{2-2\beta}{3}}\tau\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|b^{\prime}(y)|^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy+\epsilon^{\frac{4+2\beta}{3}}\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy\\
&\lesssim\tau\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(b(y)-\lambda)^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy+\epsilon^{\frac{4+2\beta}{3}}C(\tau)\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy,\end{split}

(3.14)

and that for sufficiently small
\sigma_{0}\in(0,1)
and
\alpha\geq-\sigma_{0}\epsilon^{\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}
,

\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy+|\alpha|\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\lesssim|k(z,z)|+\sigma_{0}\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(b(y)-\lambda)^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy.

(3.15)

Thus combining (
3.11
) and (
3.14
)-(
3.15
), we obtain that

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(b(y)-\lambda)^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\lesssim|b(z)-\lambda||k(z,z)|+\epsilon^{\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}|k(z,z)|,

(3.16)

which further implies that

\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(b(y)-\lambda)^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\lesssim\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}(b(z)-\lambda)\rangle|k(z,z)|.

(3.17)

Then, in view of (
3.15
) and (
3.16
), we can get the desired bounds (
3.6
) using the inequality

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(I)}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}(I)}|I|^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\|f^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(I)}|I|^{\frac{1}{2}}

(3.18)

for an interval
I
, as follows.

If
\alpha\lesssim\epsilon^{\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}
, we choose

I=\left\{y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}:\epsilon^{-\frac{1-\beta}{3}}|y-z|\leq\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}(\lambda-b(z))\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right\},\quad|I|\approx\epsilon^{\frac{1-\beta}{3}}\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}(\lambda-b(z))\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

If
\alpha\gg\epsilon^{\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}
, we choose
I=\left\{y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}:\epsilon^{-\frac{1-\beta}{3}}|y-z|\leq\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\alpha\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right\}
,
|I|\approx\epsilon^{\frac{1-\beta}{3}}\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\alpha\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.
This concludes the proof of case (2) of Lemma
3.1
.

The cases (1) and (3) follow from similar arguments.
Indeed the non-degenerate case (1), we obtain (
3.7
) by just applying
\beta=0
into (
3.6
), if additionally
|\lambda|\lesssim 1
. If
|\lambda|\gg 1
, then there is no need to use the spectral gap inequality (
3.12
), and (
3.16
) with
\beta=0
follows directly from (
3.11
).

For the viscous case, (
3.5
) follows from the same arguments as in the degenerate case if we take
\beta=\frac{1}{4}
. The proof of Lemma
3.1
is now complete.
∎

3.1.2.
The entanglement inequality

The following lemma will be used frequently in our proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For
\beta\in[0,1/4]
and non-negative cutoff function
\varphi(y)\in C^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
with
\varphi(z)=0
, there exists
c_{0}\in(0,1)
independent of
\beta
and
\varphi
such that

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\left[|\varphi^{\prime}|^{2}-c_{0}^{2}\epsilon^{-\frac{2-2\beta}{3}}\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}(b(y)-\lambda)\rangle|\varphi|^{2}\right]|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\geq 0.

(3.19)

Remark 3.1
.

The statement of our entanglement inequality is the same as that in
[
41
]
. However, our proof is much more flexible since our method here does not require the separation into regions where
\lambda-b(y)
keeps its sign. In particular, we no longer need to use the local to global energy inequality, see section 3.2 of
[
41
]
, which is essential in
[
41
]
but is somewhat cumbersome.

Proof.

We provide the detailed proof only for the most complicated case when
\lambda\in b(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
. The proof of the case
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\backslash b(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
is similar and easier.

We first introduce a real-valued test function
\phi(y)
which is a standard regularization of the jump function
\text{sgn}(b(y)-\lambda)
on the scale of
\epsilon^{\frac{1-\beta}{3}}
, the natural length scale at
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
with
\lambda=b(y)
, and which satisfies the following properties:

(1)

|\phi^{\prime}(y)|\lesssim\epsilon^{-\frac{1-\beta}{3}}
;

(2)

|\phi(y)-\text{sgn}(b(y)-\lambda)||b(y)-\lambda|\lesssim\epsilon^{\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}
.

Multiplying
\varphi^{2}(y)\overline{k(y,z)}
to (
3.1
) and integrating over
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we have

\begin{split}-\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varphi^{2}(y)|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy-\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}2\varphi^{\prime}(y)\varphi(y)\overline{k(y,z)}\partial_{y}k(y,z)dy-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varphi^{2}(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\\
+i\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(b(y)-\lambda)\varphi^{2}(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy=0.\end{split}

(3.20)

By taking the real part and applying the Hölder inequality, we get that

\alpha\epsilon^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varphi^{2}(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy+\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varphi^{2}(y)|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\varphi^{\prime}(y)|^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy.

(3.21)

We also multiply
\varphi^{2}(y)\phi(y)\overline{k(y,z)}
to (
3.1
) and integrate over
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
to get that

\begin{split}-\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varphi^{2}(y)\phi(y)|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy-\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\left(2\varphi(y)\varphi^{\prime}(y)\phi(y)+\varphi^{2}(y)\phi^{\prime}(y)\right)\overline{k(y,z)}\partial_{y}k(y,z)dy\\
-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varphi^{2}(y)\phi(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy+i\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(b(y)-\lambda)\varphi^{2}(y)\phi(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy=0.\end{split}

(3.22)

Taking the imaginary part, we get that

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(b(y)-\lambda)\varphi^{2}(y)\phi(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\\
&\leq\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\Big[2|\varphi(y)||\varphi^{\prime}(y)||\phi(y)|+\varphi^{2}(y)|\phi^{\prime}(y)|\Big]\big|k(y,z)\big||\partial_{y}k(y,z)|dy\end{split}

(3.23)

Thus by the construction of the function
\phi(y)
, we conclude that

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|b(y)-\lambda|\varphi^{2}(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(b(y)-\lambda)\Big[\text{sgn}(b(y)-\lambda)-\phi(y)\Big]\varphi^{2}(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy+\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}(b(y)-\lambda)\phi(y)\varphi^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\epsilon^{\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\varphi^{2}(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy+\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\Big[2|\varphi(y)||\varphi^{\prime}(y)||\phi(y)|+\varphi^{2}(y)|\phi^{\prime}(y)|\Big]|k(y,z)||\partial_{y}k(y,z)|dy.\end{split}

(3.24)

By the spectral gap inequality that for any
\tau\in(0,1)
, a corresponding
C(\tau)\in(1,\infty)
, and any
h\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
,

\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|h(y)|^{2}dy\leq\tau\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|b(y)-\lambda||h(y)|^{2}dy+C(\tau)\epsilon^{\frac{2-2\beta}{3}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\partial_{y}h(y)|^{2}dy,

(3.25)

together with (
3.24
), we get that

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|b(y)-\lambda|\varphi^{2}(y)|k(y,z)|^{2}dy\\
&\lesssim\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}|\varphi^{\prime}(y)|^{2}|k(y,z)|^{2}+\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}\varphi^{2}(y)|\partial_{y}k(y,z)|^{2}dy.\end{split}

(3.26)

Using the spectral gap inequality (
3.25
), the bounds (
3.21
) when
\alpha\geq-\sigma_{0}\epsilon^{\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}
with a sufficiently small
\sigma_{0}\in(0,1)
, and (
3.26
), we then obtain the desired inequality (
3.19
).

∎

3.1.3.
Point-wise bounds for the kernel

Finally, we prove our main conclusion Proposition
3.1
.

We introduce the length scale
Q(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
which will be used frequently in our argument. Notice that there is a slight abuse of notation and
x
in this section is not to be confused with the
x
variable for the non-square torus
\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

We first define for
x\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}},\beta\in[0,1/4]
,

Q(x;\beta):=\frac{\epsilon^{\frac{1-\beta}{3}}}{\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}(b(x)-\lambda)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Since
\beta
, determined by
\lambda\in b(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
, is fixed, we also use the following shortened notation
Q(x)
. In the non-degenerate case,
Q(x)=Q(x;0)
; in the degenerate case,
Q(x)=Q(x;\beta)
; and in the viscous case,
Q(x)=Q(x;\frac{1}{4})
.

When
|y-z|\lesssim Q(z)
, notice here
\{|y-z|\leq Q(z)\}\subset I
where
I
is taken from the proof of Lemma
3.1
. Thus by Lemma
3.1
,

|k(y,z)|\lesssim\epsilon^{-1}Q(z)\cdot e^{0},

(3.27)

which satisfies (
3.2
)-(
3.4
) naturally.

When
Q(z)\ll|z-y|
, we will apply the entanglement inequality to get the bound. Without loss of generality, we assume
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
is in the positive direction to
z
, counter-clockwisely.

Let
\Gamma_{x_{1},x_{2}}
be the arc on
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
in the counter-clockwise direction. We assume that

\int_{\Gamma_{z+Q(z),y}}Q^{-1}(x)\,dx\geq\int_{\Gamma_{y,z-Q(z)}}Q^{-1}(x)\,dx.

(3.28)

Then we can find
y_{\ast}\in[z+Q(z),y]
such that

m_{\ast}:=c_{0}\int_{\Gamma_{z+Q(z),y_{\ast}}}Q^{-1}(x)\,dx=c_{0}\int_{\Gamma_{y+Q(y),z-Q(z)}}Q^{-1}(x)\,dx.

(3.29)

Consider the points

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=z-Q(z),

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=z+Q(z),

\displaystyle A_{3}

\displaystyle=y_{\ast},

\displaystyle A_{4}

\displaystyle=y+Q(y).

(3.30)

Using (
3.1.3
) we choose
\varphi
for the entanglement inequality (
3.19
) satisfying the following conditions:

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(x)=c_{0}Q^{-1}(x)\varphi(x)\quad\text{ for }x\in[A_{2},A_{3}];

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(x)=-c_{0}Q^{-1}(x)\varphi(x)\quad\text{ for }x\in[A_{4},A_{1}],\quad\varphi(A_{1})=\varphi(A_{2})=1;

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime\prime}(x)\equiv 0,\quad\text{ for }x\in[A_{3},A_{4}]\cup[A_{1},z)\cup(z,A_{2}],\quad\varphi(z)=0.

(3.31)

We have from (
3.1.3
) that there exists
c_{1}\in(0,1)
, such that

\varphi(A_{3})=e^{m_{\ast}}\gtrsim\exp\left[c_{1}\Big\langle\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}\alpha,\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}(b(y)-\lambda),\epsilon^{-\frac{1+2\beta}{3}}(b(z)-\lambda)\Big\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}|y-z|\epsilon^{-\frac{1-\beta}{3}}\right].

(3.32)

Using the entanglement inequality (
3.19
) we obtain that

\int_{y}^{y+Q(y)}e^{2m_{\ast}}Q^{-2}(x)|k(x,z)|^{2}dx\lesssim\int_{z-Q(z)}^{z+Q(z)}Q^{-2}(x)|k(x,z)|^{2}\,dx,

(3.33)

which together with equation (
3.1
) implies the desired point-wise estimates. The proof of Proposition
3.1
is now complete.

3.2.
Applications of the kernel estimates

In this section we prove several bounds concerning the operator

A_{\Theta}:=\frac{\nu}{k}\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))-\alpha.

(3.34)

In the above, we use the same assumptions and notations as in proposition
1.1
and recall that
k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,\infty)
. We also used the composite symbol
\Theta:=(k,\alpha,\nu,\lambda)
for the ease of notations.

Assume that
j\in\{1,2\},\gamma\in[7/4,2)
.
For
\lambda\in\Sigma_{j,\delta_{0}},y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\epsilon:=\nu/k\in(0,1/8)
, we denote
\Lambda:=(\lambda,\alpha,\epsilon)
and recall from (
1.38
) that

d_{j,k}(\Lambda):=\delta(\Lambda)\wedge\frac{1}{|k|},\quad\varrho_{j,k}(y;\Lambda):=\varrho_{j}(y;\Lambda)\wedge\frac{1}{|k|}.

(3.35)

Then we have the following bounds.

Lemma 3.3
.

For any
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{1}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3})>0
sufficiently small, such that for
0<\nu<\epsilon_{1},k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,\infty)
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, and
1/4\geq\alpha\geq-\sigma_{0}|\nu/k|^{1/2}
as in Proposition
1.1
, we have the following bounds for

w=\varrho_{j}^{\sigma_{1}}(y;\Lambda)\varrho_{j,k}^{\sigma_{2}}(y;\Lambda)\big(i(\lambda-b(y))+|\alpha|+|\nu/k|^{1/2}+|\nu/k|^{1/3}|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|^{1/3}\big)^{\sigma_{3}}

(3.36)

and
p\in[1,\infty]
,
h\in L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
.

(i) For
\lambda\in\Sigma_{j,\delta_{0}},m\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[0,1]
,
j\in\{1,2\}
,

\begin{split}&\left\|w(y)\partial_{y}^{m}A_{\Theta}^{-1}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\\
&\lesssim_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}}\left\|\frac{w(y)}{(L_{j}(y;\Lambda))^{m}\big(i(\lambda-b(y))+|\alpha|+|\nu/k|^{1/2}+|\nu/k|^{1/3}|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|^{1/3}\big)}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})},\end{split}

(3.37)

where for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\geq|\nu/k|^{1/2}
,

L_{j}(y;\Lambda):=\frac{|\nu/k|^{1/3}|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|^{-1/6}}{\langle|\nu/k|^{-1/3}|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|^{-1/3}(\lambda-b(y)),|\nu/k|^{-1/3}|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|^{-1/3}\alpha\rangle^{1/2}},

(3.38)

and for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,
|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|<|\nu/k|^{1/2}
,

L_{j}(y;\Lambda):=|\nu/k|^{1/4}\langle|\nu/k|^{-1/2}(\lambda-b(y)),|\nu/k|^{-1/2}\alpha\rangle^{-1/2};

(3.39)

In addition, we have for
m\in\{1,2\}
the bounds

\begin{split}&\left\|w(y)\partial_{y}^{m}A_{\Theta}^{-1}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\\
&\lesssim_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},m,p}\left\|\frac{w(y)}{(L_{j}(y;\Lambda))^{m-1}\big(i(\lambda-b(y))+|\alpha|+|\nu/k|^{1/2}+|\nu/k|^{1/3}|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|^{1/3}\big)}\partial_{y}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\left\|\frac{w(y)\varrho_{j}(y;\Lambda)}{(L_{j}(y;\Lambda))^{m-1}\big(i(\lambda-b(y))+|\alpha|+|\nu/k|^{1/2}+|\nu/k|^{1/3}|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|^{1/3}\big)^{2}}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}.\end{split}

(3.40)

In particular, if
h\equiv 0
for
|y-y_{j\ast}|\leq\delta(\lambda)
then we have for
m\in\{1,2\}
,

\begin{split}&\left\|\varrho_{j}^{\sigma_{1}}(y;\Lambda)\varrho_{j,k}^{\sigma_{2}}(y;\Lambda)\partial_{y}^{m}A_{\Theta}^{-1}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\\
&\lesssim_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},m,p}\left\|\frac{\varrho_{j}^{\sigma_{1}-2}(y;\Lambda)\varrho_{j,k}^{\sigma_{2}}(y;\Lambda)}{(L_{j}(y;\Lambda))^{m-1}}\partial_{y}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}+\left\|\frac{\varrho_{j}^{\sigma_{1}-3}(y;\Lambda)\varrho_{j,k}^{\sigma_{2}}(y;\Lambda)}{(L_{j}(y;\Lambda))^{m-1}}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}.\end{split}

(3.41)

(ii) For
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\big(\cup_{j\in\{1,2\}}\Sigma_{j,\delta_{0}}\big)
,
m\in\{0,1\}
,

\left\|\partial_{y}^{m}A_{\Theta}^{-1}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\lesssim\left\|(L_{j}(y;\Lambda))^{-m}\big(i(\lambda-b(y))+|\alpha|+|\nu/k|^{1/3}\big)^{-1}h\right\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})},

(3.42)

where

L(y;\Lambda):=|\nu/k|^{1/3}\langle|\nu/k|^{-1/3}(\lambda-b(y)),|\nu/k|^{-1/3}\alpha\rangle^{-1/2}.

(3.43)

Proof.

The main idea of the proof is to keep track of the correct length scales, and to show that the kernel for
A_{\Theta}^{-1}
always defines a smaller length scale on which it decays when compared with the weights. Therefore we can treat
A_{\Theta}^{-1}
as a local operator when proving these weighted estimates.

We focus only on the proof of (i), with (ii) being similar. Denote
N(y,z)
,
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
as the kernel of
A_{\Theta}^{-1}
, i.e. for any
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
and
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

A_{\Theta}^{-1}h(y)=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}N(y,z)h(z)\,dz.

(3.44)

It follows from Proposition
3.1
that for some
c_{0}\in(0,1)
,
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

|N(y,z)|\lesssim|\nu/k|^{-1}L_{j}(y;\Lambda)e^{-c_{0}\big(L^{-1}_{j}(y;\Lambda)+L^{-1}_{j}(z;\Lambda)\big)|y-z|}.

(3.45)

Denote for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

w_{\ast}(y):=i(\lambda-b(y))+|\alpha|+|\nu/k|^{1/2}+|\nu/k|^{1/3}|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|^{1/3}.

(3.46)

We note that for

w\in\{\varrho_{j}^{\sigma_{1}},\varrho_{j,k}^{\sigma_{2}},L_{j}(\cdot;\Lambda),w_{\ast}\}

(3.47)

and any
\sigma>0
,
y,z\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

w(y)\lesssim_{\sigma}w(z)\,e^{\sigma\big(L^{-1}_{j}(y;\Lambda)+L^{-1}_{j}(z;\Lambda)\big)|y-z|}.

(3.48)

Using (
3.48
), we can bound for any
w
as in (
3.47
),

\begin{split}&\Big\|w(y)A_{\Theta}^{-1}h(y)\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\\
&\lesssim\Big\|w(y)\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}N(y,z)h(z)dz\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}\\
&\lesssim_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\Big\||\nu/k|^{-1}L_{j}(y;\Lambda)w_{\ast}(y)\int_{\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}}e^{-c_{1}\big(L^{-1}_{j}(y;\Lambda)+L^{-1}_{j}(z;\Lambda)\big)|y-z|}w_{\ast}^{-1}(z)w(z)|h(z)|dz\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})}.\end{split}

(3.49)

To complete the proof of (
3.37
), we divide
\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
as the union of intervals
\mathcal{I}_{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,N]
for some integer
N\geq 1
, where the intervals
\mathcal{I}_{i}
only intersect at the endpoints with each other and
|\mathcal{I}_{i}|\approx L_{j}(y;\Lambda)\approx\ell_{i}
for
y\in\mathcal{I}_{i}
.

Then using (
3.49
) we can bound for suitable constants
c_{2},c_{3}\in(0,1)
and each
i\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,N]
,

\begin{split}&\Big\|w(y)A_{\Theta}^{-1}h(y)\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{I}_{i})}\\
&\lesssim_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\Big\|\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{i^{\prime}}}\ell_{i}^{-1}e^{-c_{2}(\ell_{i}^{-1}+\ell_{i^{\prime}}^{-1})|y-z|}w_{\ast}^{-1}(z)w(z)|h(z)|dz\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{I}_{i})}\\
&\lesssim_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\Big\|\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}\int_{\mathcal{I}_{i^{\prime}}}\ell_{i}^{-1}e^{-c_{3}|i-i^{\prime}|}e^{-c_{3}\ell_{i}^{-1}|y-z|}w_{\ast}^{-1}(z)w(z)|h(z)|dz\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{I}_{i})}\\
&\lesssim_{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}\sum_{i^{\prime}=1}^{N}e^{-c_{3}|i-i^{\prime}|}\big\|w_{\ast}^{-1}(z)w(z)h\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{I}_{i^{\prime}})}.\end{split}

(3.50)

The desired bound (
3.37
) follows from (
3.50
).

The desired bounds (
3.41
) follow from the bounds (
3.37
) and the equation for
y\in\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}}
,

\Big[\frac{\nu}{k}\partial_{y}^{2}+i(\lambda-b(y))-\alpha\Big]\partial_{y}u(y)=\partial_{y}h(y)+ib^{\prime}(y)u(y),

(3.51)

where

u=A_{\Theta}^{-1}h.

(3.52)

∎

The above lemmas are very useful for treating
A_{\Theta}^{-1}
away from the singular region when
|\lambda-b(y)|\gtrsim\delta(\lambda)
. In the viscous layer when
|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\lesssim|\nu/k|^{1/2}
, we can use the viscous term
(\nu/k)\partial_{y}^{2}
to gain regularity without much loss. To deal with the singular behavior in the remaining case when
|\nu/k|^{1/2}\lesssim|\lambda-b(y)|\lesssim\delta(\lambda)
, we rely on the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

For any
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
\epsilon_{1}=\epsilon_{1}(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})>0
sufficiently small, such that for
0<\nu<\epsilon_{1},k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[1,\infty)
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
,
-\sigma_{0}|\nu/k|^{1/2}\leq\alpha\ll|\nu/k|^{1/3}|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|^{1/3}
, where
\sigma_{0}
is from Propostion
3.1
, the following statement holds.

(i) Assume that
\lambda\in\Sigma_{j,\delta_{0}}\cap b(\mathbb{T}_{\mathfrak{p}})
and
|\lambda-b(y_{j\ast})|\gg|\nu/k|^{1/2}
. In this case there exist
y^{+}_{\lambda},y^{-}_{\lambda}\in S^{j}_{3\delta}
, arranged so that
y^{+}_{\lambda}
is in the positive direction, counter-clockwise, to
y^{-}_{\lambda}
, such that

\lambda=b(y^{+}_{\lambda})=b(y^{-}_{\lambda}).

(3.53)

Choose a cut-off function
